[trialAccount t18460202-634]
634. JEREMIAH COLLINS was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Peter Stacey, about two in the night of the 20th of Jan., and stealing therein 1 tea-caddy, value 1 s.; 1 box, 6 d.; 1/2lb. weight of tea, 2 s. 1 d.; 1lb. weight of sugar, 6 d.; 1lb. weight of bacon, 8 d.; 4 sovereigns, 4 half-crowns, 25 shillings, and 10 sixpences, his property.
ELIZABETH STACEY . I live at No. 40, Church-street, Rotherhithe. I am the wife of Peter Stacey—we keep a beer shop—on the 20th of Jan., on going down stairs at night I noticed a door closed which I usually left open—I put my hand against it, and felt something against it, some resistance, something outside, and on my shaking it the handle of a broom fell—on opening the door there is a window, as you come to the bottom of the stairs, which shows light into the bar—I saw the gas alight, and saw a man standing with his back against the window, with somethings lying on the table, he appeared to see me because he ran right to the door and escaped—I think the door must have been on the jar, closed, but not latched,—I did not hear him lift the latch—he had no shoes on—he had on a round jacket, and a cap, and cord trowsers—the gas was burning in the room—I missed four sovereigns and 2 l. worth of silver—I had left the money in a box, wrapped up in a piece of paper in a closet on the stairs overnight—some articles had been removed—a tea-caddy was taken off the mantle-piece with 1/2lb. of tea in it—I had seen the prisoner at our house the night before, but do not remember seeing him for several weeks before—I will not positively swear he is the man I saw in the parlour—but I have every reason to believe he is.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Other persons were fetched to your premises, were they not? A. When he ran to the door I called "Police"—a man crossed and asked what was the matter—I was frightened and did not tell him, but ran in doors and told my son and husband—I never heard of anybody else being taken—the prisoner did not give himself up, he was taken at the Eagle, just by my house—he came to my place, and said, "Does master want me?" I said, "I don't know, you had better call again," he went to the door and I had given information about him—I did not ask why he thought master wanted him—I thought he would run away if I questioned him—I could not detain him—I went immediately to the station—I was disturbed about a quarter past two o'clock in the night.
PETER STACEY . I am the witness's husband—I was the last person up—the house was quite secured—I went to bed at twenty minutes after eleven—I fastened the back doors—I went into the yard twice after I shut the front door—the street door was quite fastened—when the alarm was given I got up—my wife said something, when I came down—I went to the back door and found it shut, but the side part of the door jamb was broken away, a few boards taken out and a hole large enough for an arm to be put through and unbolt the door—I found a pair of shoes near the door, and outside the door a chisel, which I gave the policeman—my house is in the parish of Rotherhithe.
Cross-examined. Q. Is that the name of the parish? A. Yes—I keep a beer and coffee shop.
CHARLES LACK . I am toll-collector at the Thames Tunnel—about a quarter-past two on the morning of the robbery, I saw a man come out of the prosecutor's house, without shoes—he had on a short jacket and cap—he ran away—I had not an opportunity of seeing his features—I have known the prisoner several years—it is my firm belief he is the man.
Cross-examined. Q. This was the darkest time of the night, was it? A. Yes, but there was a gas-light within eight or ten feet of the house, so I had an opportunity of seeing him—Mrs. Stacey was calling police the moment I came up to the door.
WILLIAM BRADLEY . I live in Church-street, Rotherhithe, in the same house with the prisoner—I remember having some shoes from the prisoner's mother, to take to Cuddy's—I did not see them as they were in a handkerchief—I live in Mrs. Collins's house.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not the prisoner come home at his usual hour that night? A. Yes, about half-past ten or eleven—he remained there all night, as far as I know—I saw him about nine o'clock next morning in his bed-room—he slept in the same room with his mother.
WILLIAM COLLINS . I am a shoemaker, I know these shoes—I half-soled them for the prisoner, my work is on them—he brought them himself and fetched them away—I have reason to believe they are the shoes.
Cross-examined. Q. You think there is some of your work on them? A. Yes ( pointing it out)—I can swear to the work—they are the shoes I have repaired twice for him—I know the soles, they are nailed, as he ordered them, as he walked and I knew his wear—I was a witness in the New Court twelve years ago, against a boy for robbing my master—I was never in custody—I did not see the prisoner after the robbery, till he was taken.
SAMUEL ANDREWS ( police-constable N 264.) I received these shoes from Stacey.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you notice of this robbery? A. I knew nothing of it, till I heard a cry of "Police"—I went with Mr. Stacey to see if a certain boy was known at home.
RICHARD HILL ( police-constable M 16.) I went to the Spread Eagle on Wednesday, the 21st of Jan., about half-past eleven, and found the prisoner in the tap-room—he denied the charge—I showed him the chisel—he said "It is mine," I told him to be careful what he said, as it would be used in evidence against him—and he then said, he knew nothing of the chisel.
Cross-examined. Q. How did you get the chisel? A. I had it from Mr. Stacey—the prosecutor's is a well conducted house, for all I know—I did not hear that the prisoner was going to give himself up.
JANE COLLINS . I took the shoes home to the prisoner, after my husband had worked on them.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18460202-635]
635. WILLIAM WILSON and JOHN SOUTHGATE were indicted for a burglary in the dwelling-house of William Spencer, about two o'clock in the night of the 10th of Jan., and stealing therein, 1 ham, value 7 s.; 3lbs. weight of pork, 2 s.; and 2 dead fowls, 3 s. 6 d.; his goods; to which
WILSON pleaded GUILTY of Larceny. Confined Six Months.
WILLIAM SPENCER . I am a butcher, and live in Brierley-place, Wandsworth, in the parish of Lambeth, and keep the house. On the 10th of Jan. I fastened up my house at night—I went into my back garden, between nine and ten o'clock in the morning, and saw the cellar flap was removed from its original situation—I had heard a rumbling noise in the night, which awoke me—I rose in my bed and listened—my wife thought it was the children—I laid down, and heard a second noise—I went down stairs—all was quiet—I went up again, but on seeing the cellar flap I thought I had been robbed—the flap had been nailed down, and had a window inside it—that was raised, and taken away, the whole flap and frame together—they drew the ends of it out of the wall by prizing it, I imagine—I missed two dead fowls, some mutton chops, some pork, a ham, and part of a plum-pudding—the fowls were kept in the house, under the cellar stairs.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTONE. Q. Is there any tenant in the house but yourself? A. No—the flap had been fastened into the wall—the cellar door was fastened.
JOHN MAY ( police-constable V 281.) I apprehended Southgate—I told him what I took him for—(I did not say Wilson was in custody)—he asked me if the other one had split —I said, "No"—on the way to the station I told him Wilson said he took the shovel down, which the cellar flap was ripped up with—he said that was wrong, the shovel was laying on the dung-hill when they went down—after they got to the station I told him I must have his boots off to try to some footmarks at the back of the premises—he said they were not the boots he had on on the Saturday night—I had found these boots at his father's, down stairs—I received a pair of boots from his father, and compared them with several footmarks in Mr. Spencer's garden—it appeared to me they would make similar marks on the ground—I did not make any marks, but tried them on those which had been made.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it true or false that Wilson had not split? A. Why, it was false—I was not aware at the time that he knew Wilson was in custody—I knew Wilson had said something—I told him a falsehood—I had no object in view—I intended to state here what he said—I produce the boots.
JOHN DEMPSEY ( police-constable V 268.) I was at the station when Southgate was brought in—no threat or promise was held out to him—I did not tell him that what he said would be used, for or against him—he said it was no use for him to deny it now, as the other one had told all about it; he might as well tell what he knew about it—he said, "He wants to throw it all on me, and he has done as much of it as I have; he pulled his shoes off, not to leave any marks in ihe place, and he found the shovel what I opened the place with."
Cross-examined. Q. Had you no conversation at all with him? A. Not a word—no remark came from me at all—nobody had been speaking about it at all—May had taken him into custody, and left him at the station before I came there.
WILLIAM SPENCER re-examined. Q. Is there a communication from the parlour to the passage in which the things hung? A. Yes—there was furniture in the parlour—that was not displaced.
SOUTHGATE — GUILTY of Stealing only. Confined Six Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18460202-636]
636. WILLIAM SIBLEY was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 30 s.; 1 waistcoat, 16 s.; and 1 pair of trowsers, 15 s.; the goods of George Ford; to which he pleaded
GUILTY .— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Baron Rolfe.
[trialAccount t18460202-637]
637. SAMUEL OVERY was indicted for that he, on the 13th of Nov., feloniously did assault Celia Overy, and strike her on the head, thereby causing her a bodily injury dangerous to life, with intent'to murder her.—Two other COUNTS, for wounding, with intent to do grievous bodily harm.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
CELIA OVERY . I am about thirteen years old, and live with the prisoner, who is my father, at Dockhcad—I have a mother-in-law. On the day this happened I had my mother's baby—she went out, and left it at home—I cleaned the fire-place, and stirred the fire with the poker, and left it in the fire—it was in the morning—the baby was playing on the floor—it cannot walk, but can crawl—my father was in the room—he had not been out—he got the poker out of the fire, drove me into the middle of the room, and put my two hands together in his right hand—he hit me on the forehead with the poker, which was in his left hand, a good many times—he did not say a word to me—I had not spoken a word to him—he hit me on the head when he moved me into the middle of the room—he had been sitting on a chair by the fire before that—he works at home, at shoemaking, but not in that room—he has not been at work since Whit Monday, half a year ago—I do not know what happened after I was struck—when I came to my senses I was in the hospital—this is the poker now produced—I remember it well—here is the mark where I put it into the fire.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. Do you remember after your father struck you, whether he danced round about you? A. No, he fell down on the top of the stairs—it is an up-stair room—my father was sometimes kind to me, and sometimes very cross—I ran into the yard, and exclaimed, "Oh my poor father,"—I remember that very well—I had not done anything to make him angry—he said nothing to me before he struck me—he has been very ill in the head since Whit-Monday.
COURT. Q. What do you mean by being very ill in the head? A. I do not know—he did not always keep his bed, he sometimes got up and sat by the fire—I do not know whether he talked sensibly—I never saw a person mad or beside themselves.
LOUISA JONES . I am the wife of Thomas Jones, a leather-dresser, and live next door to the prisoner. On the 13th of Nov., between ten and eleven o'clock, I heard a noise, which induced me to go to hit home—the front door was open—I went up the stairs, and found the prisoner on the top landing of the first floor—there are two rooms on the floor—he occupies the house—the sitting-room door wan open—when I got up, the prisoner came out of the back room on the same floor—the door was shut—Celia opened the door of the back room, and came out with the child in her arms—she had to step over her father's hand as she came out—she was holding a handkerchief to her head, which she moved, and the blood flowed copiously—sho said nothing to me, but went down two or three stairs—then a witness took the child from her—I followed her to the yard, and saw her bleeding very much—I went for a doctor—I did not hear her say anything—when I returned she was taken away—I heard the prisoner groaning at different times as he lay on the
landing—I believe he had not been at work for sometime before this happened.
Cross-examined. Q. When you went for the surgeon you left the girl in the yard in care of somebody? A. Yes.
MARY STEDMAN . I am the wife of Marshall Stedman, and live next door to the prisoner. I went to his house at the same time as Mrs. Jones—I followed her up stairs—I saw Celia coming down stairs, with the child in her arms, and a wound on her forehead—I took the child from her—she then went into the yard—we washed her head, wrapped it up, and sent her to the hospital—in about a quarter of an hour she was quite sensible, and said her father had done it with a poker—I saw the father lying on the landing.
MARY ANN CLARKE . I am the wife of John Clarke, a coal-porter, and live in the same house as the prisoner, in the front parlour—I heard a noise in the prisoner's room, which is the front room, over mine—I went to the foot of the stairs, and called out, "Mr. Overy, what are you doing?"—no answer was made—he came out of the front room, towards the stairs, and threw himself down on the landing—I cannot say whether he saw me—I saw Celia come out of the front room, bleeding from the head, and go into the back room—I went to the door, and called "Murder"—I did not see her afterwards—I have frequent opportunities of seeing the prisoner—I saw him every day, unless he was in the country, where he was several times during the summer—he was generally at home—he has not worked while I have been there—he has been ill all the time—I do not know that he ever kept his bed all day through—I do not know what was the matter with him—he had a doctor from the dispensary—he used to go to him—he has not been able to work during the eight months I have been there.
GEORGE HENRY EDWARDS . I am dresser at Guy's-hospital. On the 13th of Nov. Celia was brought there with three wounds on her head—one just by the orbit, and two below it—the one above the eye-brow was the most severe, and exposed the bone, which was fractured—the skin was divided—all wounds in the head, are dangerous, especially compound fractures of the scull, which this was—I think this poker would inflict such injury—she was partly sensible, in a state of collapse, but could answer Questions when aroused—then the would partially relapse—she has been in the hospital ever since, as an out-patient.
DONALD MURRAY ( policeman.) I apprehended the prisoner on the 19th of Nov., at his house—I do not know why he was not taken before—I had a warrant, which I read to him—he was then in bed—it was between four and five o'clock in the afternoon—I told him I came to take him on that warrant—it charged him with assaulting Celia, his daughter, with a poker—he made no reply—he said he was very ill, and not able to be removed out of his bed—this was on the 18th—I got Mr. Martin, the parish surgeon, to see him—he wrote me a certificate on the 19th, and I took the prisoner away.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe he was removed to the infirmary? A. I took him to goal in a cab, and had strict orders to see him placed in the infirmary—I do not recollect hearing him say anything before the Magistrate.
MR. PARRY called
CHARLES MAYMOT . I am a surgeon of Horsemonger-lane-goal. The prisoner has been under my care there about two months—he was placed in the infirmary immediately he came there—he was then very weak, and in a state of fever, which I should think he had been labouring under for some time—during the time he was with me, there were head symptoms—he had delirium occasionally, and dysentery—a discharge of blood from the bowels—that sometimes occurs with delirium—it is difficult to determine, but I imagine the disease of the bowels was of long standing, from his account, and
very likely it might be so—he remained in that state nearly all the time he was with me—he has been getting better—I have heard the evidence—he might have committed this act in a state of delirium—the fever produced by his disease would be likely to cause such a kind of delirium as might lead him to do an act of this kind.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Delirium once established, you would not be surprised at any act of violence? A. No—I think the discharge from his bowels took place the day after he came in—I had given him no medicine—the discharge indicated great debility, accompanied by the low fever which would undoubtedly he accompanied by irregular circulation—if that took a direction upward, delirium might come on at once.
WILLIAM BAKER . I live in Red-cross-street. I am secretary to the Old Friends Benefit Society—the prisoner was a member, and is now—I have been paying him sick money for about six months, weekly, and visited him often—he has mentioned to me several times that he has been very bad in his head.
JOHN OSBORNE . I live in Harper-street, New Kent-road. I have known the prisoner about ten years—he was ill about four years ago of a fever, something of a brain fever—he was rather delirious—I recollect Dr. Baldwin of the Dispensary ordering a blister on the back of his head—my wife fetched it.
NOT GUILTY , being insane at the time.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18460202-639]
639. ANDREW CLARK and THOMAS MORTISHEAD were indicted for stealing 213lbs. weight of lead, value 1 l. 10 s., the goods of William Milman, and fixed to a certain building; and THOMAS HICKMAN for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD ARTHUR ( police-sergeant L 27.) On the night of the 12th of Jan., about half-past eight o'clock, I saw Clark and Mortishead drawing a truck along Glasshouse-street—they stopped at the door of the prisoner Hickman, which is a marine-store shop—I went there and Mortishead and Clark were in the shop—I went into the shop—Hickman had been out to the truck, and was going in with a piece of lead in his arms, from the truck—Clark was coming out—I told him he must account where that lead came from—he said the other man would account for that—I then asked Mortishead to give me an account of it—he said he could do that, but he did not—Hickman carried the lead he had into the shop, and put it on some rags—Clark then iaid hold of the handle of the truck, and was going away with it—I told him he must not take it till he gave me an account of the lead—he then threw down the handle of the truck and ran away—Mortishead came out of the shop, and turned to the right—I brought him back into the shop—I saw the lead which Hickman had carried into the shop—it was on the rags still—I saw a scale there full of lead—I told Mortishead he must give me an account of that lead—he said he could do that, and said the other prisoner would account for that when he came
back—I asked him repeatedly, and got the same answer—while I was saying that, Hickman went into the parlour, leaving the lead on the rags—he came but two or three times while I was thus questioning Mortishead, and told me to take Mortishead and the lead out of his shop, or he would throw it out in the street—I sent for another officer—I took Mortishead and the lead to the station—when I got Mortishead there—he said the other man employed him, and said he would give him a pot of beer to help him draw the truck—he did not say anything about the lead—it weighed 213lbs.—I caught Clarke the next day—I asked him if he recollected running away from me—he said I must be mistaken, it was not him—I am sure it was him.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. When you Went to the shop Clarke was coming out? A. Yes—when I asked Mortishead where he got the property, he said he could tell me—he did not then tell tile that the other man had asked him to help him.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. After Clarke had run away, and Mortishead wanted to go away, then Hickman said he would not have anything to do with the lead? A. Yes—I did not take Hickman then—I found him at home the next morning.
WILLIAM ATLEE ( police-constable, L 110.) I was called into the Shop by Arthur, and I heard Hickman threaten to throw the lead out of the shop—I took charge of the lead, and put it into the truck—this is the lead—I went to the premises of Mr. Milman, in Fore-street, Lambeth—I found lead missing from the roof—I compared this lead with what remained on the roof—it appeared to have been torn off—it matched exactly.
RICHARD ARTHUR re-examined. Q. The scale was full of lead? A. Yes—it was the lead which had come out of the truck—I did not see it put into the scale—it was like the lead that was in the truck.
WILLIAM ATLEE re-examined. Q. I heard Mortishead say at the station that Clarke had asked him to help him push the truck down.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. You were in the shop with the other officer? A. Yes—I was not there when Mortishead was asked the question.
FRIDERICK MILLER . I live in Glasshouse-street—I am a coal-dealer, and let out trucks—on the evening of the 12th of Jan. Mortishead came, at eight o'clock, to hire a truck for an hour—he paid me, and I let it him—that was the track that I found in possession of the police—I knew Mortisbtad—he has worked at the London Gas Works, and ha has been employed by Mr. Brown.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. Has he been in the habit of hiring trucks of you? A. I never let it to him before—he told my wife he wanted to move some things for a widow.
WILLIAM MILMAN . I am a coal-factor—I have a wharf In Lower fore-street, Lambeth—on the 16th of Jan. I was spoken to by a policeman, and the next morning I examined the roof of my wharf—I missed a great quantity of lead from there—I have had to replace 7cwt—I have seen one of the prisoners about the premises.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. Are they going on with this building? A. No—I use it for my business—no one sleept on the premises—it adjoins the wharfs—there is no watchman—the building is locked up every night—it was not broken open, to my knowledge—I have seven, eight, or nine men there—they go to work early, not very late—they leave at all hours—I have no trap-door connected with the roof—it is a low neighbourhood—there are not many lights there.
JOSEPH BONE . I am a builder—I repaired the roof of the prosecutor's
warehouse in Dec. last—I saw the policeman there fit the lead which he found—it was part of the lead which I had repaired the roof with.
Clark's Defence. I was going down Princes-street; and met Mortishead; he asked me to give him a help with his truck down to George-street; he took it down to the store; he and I took it in; the policeman came and said, "What have you got here?" Mortishead said, "Some lead, and I can giv an account of it;" Hickman never came out of the shop at all; Mortishead said he would tell when he came back where he got it; and when I saw him go away, I ran away, because I thought perhaps it was not right; I went to work the next day, and my master sent me at half-past four o'clock for a piece of plank; I met the policeman at the Marsh-gate, in coloured clothes; he asked me if I remembered meeting him the evening before; I said, "No," because I was not quite certain of the man; he told me to go down to the station—if I was guilty of stealing the lead, would I have gone to work the next day within 200 yards of the place?
RICHARD ARTHUR re-examined. I found him at work by the Marsh-gate, in the Westminster-road—he was taking a plank to Mr. Chambers—that is about 150 yards from Hickman's shop.
CLARKE — GUILTY . Aged 27.
MORTISHEAD — GUILTY . Aged 21.
Confined Nine Months.
HICKMAN — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460202-640]
640. WILLIAM SPINKS and THOMAS GORE were indicted for stealing 150 tails, called foreign docks, value 6 d., and 3lbs. weight of hair, 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Cleeve Woodward Hooper, and another, their masters.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
CLEEVE WOODWARD HOOPER . I am in partnership with my brother—we are leather-factors, in Seething-lane—we formerly carried on business as tanners in Bermondsey—a quantity of tails and hair were removed from Bermondsey to Leadenhall-street and from thence to Seething-lane—I let a warehouse where some of this hair and tails were on the 6th of Jan., and my foreman gave orders to have it cleared out—the prisoners have been in my service for fourteen years—none of these things were allowed to them as perquisites—I did not allow them, on the 8th of Jan., to take any of these tails—the hair produced corresponds with the rest that I have—these are the tails of hides, cut off in a dry state.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know that there is amongst tanners a custom that their servants should have these docks, as they are called, as perquisites? A. No, it is a system that we have set our faces against—it is allowed in the country, but never in London.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. What is the value of those tails? A. Six pence a pound—they are sold at 3 d. sometimes—it is according to the quality—I never heard of any being sold at less than 3 d.—there is no difference between tails and docks—they are more valuable in proportion to the quantity of hair on them—these are mostly long ones—I have not said that they were not worth more than 2 s.
WILLIAM HUMPHREYS ( police-constable M 47.) I was on duty in Bermondsey about nine o'clock in the evening on the 8th Jan.—I observed the two prisoners—I followed them from one marine-store dealers to a second, and at last to a third—I there saw Gore put a bag of horse-hair into a scale—I saw Spinks take 2 s., from the marine-store dealer, and heard him say, "Two pence a pound"—I asked the prisoners to give an account of this hair—they said they brought it from their master's premises, and that it was allowed them, that
their employer was Mr. E. Hooper—I asked where he lived—they both said they did not know.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did they both say together that they did not know? A. They were both together—one said it before the other—they were in a marine-store dealer's shop when I first saw them—there was no sale effected there, and I wished to see the sale effected—they did not say in my presence that Mr. Hooper, their master, lived down by the Horns.
JOHN WHITLAMB ( police-constable M 89.) I went into the shop where the prisoners sold this hair—I heard them say it was perquisites, and we should find it perfectly correct—they afterwards stated their master was Mr. E. Hooper, but they did not know where he lived—Humphreys said he should go and see—I knew them to be in a Mr. Hooper's employ, but I did not know which—they signified that if I had gone I should have found it more easily—I asked where the place was, they said round by the Horns.
HENRY KNIGHT . I am in the prosecutor's service. On the 6th or 7th of Jan. our men removed a sack of tails and hair from a warehouse that was about to be let—I saw the sack lowered down to the ground-floor—Gore was by—the sack was full—there was a little crack in the bottom of it—it was placed in the stable—I afterwards saw some tails and hair—they were similar to what was in the sack.
CLEEVE WOODWARD HOOPER re-examined. These are principally long and good tails—I should not like to sell them under 6 d. a pound.
SPINKS — GUILTY . Aged 24.
GORE — GUILTY . Aged 26.
Recommended to mercy by the Jury. Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18460202-641]
641. JOHN ALBERT WELLER was indicted for stealing 1 gun, value 1 l., the goods of George Adams; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE ADAMS . I am a carpenter, and live in Bolton-street, Kennington. I was in the House of Correction for four months—the prisoner was there alto—he was coming out, and I asked him to call upon my wife, to let her know I was well, as there had been a report I was ill—I went home on the 19th of Oct., and my gun was gone—it cannot be found since—the prisoner went back to the House of Correction fourteen days afterwards, and when he came out I gave him in charge for stealing the gun.
HANNAH ADAMS . I am the wife of George Adams. The prisoner came to me three or four times in Oct.—I lent him some money—he came last on a Saturday in Oct., and staid all the afternoon—he said there was a knock at the door—I said there was not—I had the little child in my lap—he said, "I will go"—he went—he came back and said, "There is no one; I had better be off," and said, "I shall not see you any more for ten years"—as he went he took the gun off the sideboard in the parlour—I did not see him take it but I missed it ten minutes after he left—I had seen it ten minutes before he left—my nephew had come into the house, but he went out at the back door—I saw the gun safe when my nephew came in a little before seven o'clock—my nephew had no gun with him when he went out the back way—he said a long while, and went with me to look after the prisoner, but we did not find him—he was in the House of Correction soon afterwards—he left my house exactly at seven o'clock.
ANN BRABINS . I am the wife of Thomas Brabins. On the 18th of Oct. I was at Mrs. Adams's—the prisoner was there—about seven o'clock I had occasion to leave the house for about ten minutes to go over to my house—I was going back to Mrs. Adams's house again, and the prisoner met me—he was coining out, running as fast as he could with the gun.
Prisoner. Mrs. Adams lighted me out with a candle; if I took it she must have seen it.
WILLIAM FLOYD ( police-constable V 216.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at Reigate—( read Convicted the 8 th of April, 1845, and confined six months)—he has been summarily convicted since.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.

I am a bricklayer, and was at work at a building in the Clapham-road on the 21st of Jan.—I was coming from dinner, and met the prisoner coming out of the building with something in a handkerchief—he saw me, and he dropped it and ran—I ran and took him—I went back to the place where I saw him drop something, and found this plough—he begged me to let him go—I kept him till the carpenters came, and they detained him.
JAMES NEWMAN . I was at work at this unfinished house—I left this plough there while I went to dinner—when I came back I found the prisoner detained—this is my plough.

646. JAMES COLLINS, JOHN SCOTT, THOMAS ROBINSON , and JAMES WILLIAMS were indicted for stealing 1 jacket, value 15 s.; 1 pair of fnjwsers, 15 s.; 1 waistcoat, 15 s.; 1 pair of braces, 6 d.; and 1 knife, 6 d.; the goods of George Henderson:—I waistcoat, 12 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, 2 s.; 1 pair of braces, 6 d.; and 1 knife, 6 d.; the goods of Charles Innes, in a vessel on a navigable river catted the Thames; and that Collins had been before conricted of felony.
GEORGE HENDERSON . I am master of the brig Willams —it was lying in Church-hole, Rotherhithe—on the night of the 1st of Feb., about one o'clock, I was aroused from my cabin by the policeman—I began fo examine my clothes, and I missed the articles slated—this is my jacket, my trowters, my waistcoat and braces—my coat is still missing—they were ail safe on Saturday night.
THOMAS INNIS . I am mate of the vessel. About one o'clock on Sunday morning, the 1st of Feb., I was awoke by the policeman—I missed a pair of trowsers and a waistcoat—these are them. I saw them safe when I went to bed on Saturday night—I found the prisoner Collins in the head sheet of a coal barge, lying along side of our ship—I saw Scott on the gunwale of a ballast lighter on the opposite side of the ship.
ROBERT ARMOND . I am a seaman on board this ship. I was awoke that morning by the policeman—I found Robinson and Williams in the after-hold of our own ship, concealing themselves away as much as possible—when the prisoners were taken and put in the cabin, the officers desired them to go into the boat—they made use of very improper language and stated they would not go till they had their shoes, and Scott said he wanted his hat—Robinson and Collins had no shoes on—their shoes were found in a skiff a little further in the tier, where the clothes were found—Scott's hat was found in a coal-barge but not the barge that Collins was found in.
HENRY BARWOOD , ( Thames police-constable 37.) Shortly after one o'clock that night I was going by Church-hole tier—I heard a voice at if some persons were moving in a boat—I told my boatmen to shore astern, and I heard a person jump out of a craft—I went and found a hat in a coal-barge—I went across the vessels till I came to the William —I coord see no one there—I called out to the crew and told them fo get lights—I jumped into ft coal-barge and found Collins in the head-sheets, with no shoes on—I took him on board the William and secured him—Collins, and the rest of the prisoners were very violent, they wanted to take charge ef the cabin, and wanted to find die breadlocker—they began to sing songs.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLATINE. Q. Had you known Williams, before? A. No—I have inquired and heard nothing against him he—was very quiet—the other three were very violent—he had not his shoes off—there was 18 s. 6 d. found on him, but no stolen property.
JOHN GREGORY ( Thames police-constable 34.) I found the prosecutor's clothes in a skiff where the shoes were—Collins claimed one pair of the shoes and Robinson the other.
WILLIAM JUDGE ( Thames police inspector.) I took the prisoners—they said they would not go without their hat and shoes—I asked them why they took them off—both Collins and Robinson said "to go thieving"—Williams was very quiet.
Collins. I know nothing at all about it.
WILLIAM JUDGE re-examined. I produce a certificate of Collins's former conviction at this Court—( read convicted the 7 th of April, 1845, and confined nine months)—he is the man.

[trialAccount t18460202-647]
647. CHARLOTTE RAND was indicted for stealing 73 postage stamps, value 6 s. 1 d.; 300 sheets of writing-paper, 10 s.; 300 sheets of other paper 10 s.; 3oz. weight of wafers, 6 d.; and 129 letter envelopes, 2 s.; the goods of Thomas Cockburn and others: 41bs. weight of sugar, 2 s.; 1 1/2lbs. weight of black-lead, 4 s.; 121bs. weight of butter, 12 s.; 1 plate, 1 s. 1 soup-ladle, 2 s.; 2 dishes, 2 s.; 1lb. weight of starch, 6 d.; 2 ounces weight of spice, 6 d., 8 ounces weight vermicelli, 6 d.; 6 kitchen cloths, 2 s.; 3 boxes of lucifer matches, 6 d.; 1 knife, 1 s.; and 1 fork 1 s.; of Edward Wilson her master; and ROBERT RAND for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
EDWARD WILSON . I am secretary to the British Plate-glass Company; Mr. Thomas Cockburn is one of the proprietors, and there are several others; I live at Albion-place, Blackfriars-road, where the business of the company is carried on; the prisoner Charlotte Rand was cook in the family, and had access to all parts of the house. In consequence of information, I directed the officer to watch her, and on Monday evening the 5th of Jan, she was brought back to the door in custody—I went with her and the officer to the station, and after having been there I went with the officer to No. 32, Wellington-street, Waterloo-town, Bethnal-green—I there found Robert Rand—the officer stated to him that his wife was in custody for robbing me, and asked whether he had at any time received from her any articles that she had taken away, and whether he had any in the house—he stated certainly not, and he hoped I should be lenient with the woman; he had never received anything but a very few pieces of broken victuals—the officer said, "Well, I presume there will be no objection to searching the house?"—he assented, and we proceeded to search—in a desk in the lower room were these seventy-three postage-stamps—I cannot identify them—there were such articles amongst the property of the company—I cannot say that we have missed such—we have them in sheets—in the bed-room we found a quantity of paper and envelopes—the paper was of various qualities and descriptions, all of which are used by the company—( looking at the articles)—this kind of paper the company have from the manufacturer, at Maidstone, and this other from a dealer—we found 129 envelopes, some of which I had purposely made for the company—I think there were seven or eight screws of paper containing sugar, some loafsugar, some in powder, and some in lump—we keep our white sugar in powder—there was some black-lead, some mould candles, six or seven different bundles of scouring-paper, containing eight or nine sheets each, about 100 sheets in all; two large pans of butter, one plate, two dishes, and a soup-ladle—this is the plate—I know it; it has been with us fifteen or sixteen years—we found some kitchen cloths, some boxes of lucifer matches, and a knife and fork—I believe them to be my property and the property of the company—some of them I know.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You have not missed any of these? A. Not any postage-stamps; I missed paper—Robert Rand is a tailor, I believe—there are four rooms in his house—when we went up stairs there was a hesitation, but he assented—he said he hoped I would be as lenient as possible with his wife—he said nothing about himself—some of this paper was found in a chest of drawers—there were no women's dresses in that chest—many of these articles were found in another chest of drawers, which he asked for his keys to unlock—he said, "Mother, give me my keys, I can't open these drawers without them"—he had used the keys for the other chest of drawers, and he wanted them handed across to him—I think he had them himself first, and the mother had not them till she handed them to
him—he did not say that chest of drawers was his wife's—I think there were no women's things in them.
STEPHEN THORNTON ( police-sergeant A 20.) I was employed to watch the movements of the female prisoner—on the evening of the 5th of Jan., between six and seven o'clock, I saw her leave the premises of the British Plate-glass Company—she appeared to have something under her cloak—she went to Seacoal-lane, and was met by Braham—they went together into the lobby of a pawnbroker's shop—I took them both—I then proceeded with Mr. Wilson to the house in Bethnal-green—I there saw Robert Rand—I asked if he was the husband of Charlotte Rand, and told him his wife was in custody for robbing Mr. Wilson, and I wished to search his house—he said he had no objection, and in a box below I found seventy-three Queen's heads—I asked how they came by them, and the mother replied her son had found them—I found these other articles up stairs—the man did not give any account of them—I asked, when I found the butter and other things, where he got them from, and he said he should reserve his defence.
Cross-examined. Q. The mother was living at the house? A. Yes—I understood she was Robert Rand's mother—I believe no other woman lives there—two rooms are let out to lodgers, but I believe they are men—there were articles of female apparel in the last drawers, not in the first—I cannot say positively who produced the keys at first—it was a bundle of keys—the mother was not doing anything about finding them—she was present—he and his mother occupy the house—the candles that they were burning down stairs were mould, and I found similar ones up stairs.
AMBROSE AUSTIN . I am servant to Mr. Wilson—I know this soup-ladle, and the plate and dishes—I have seen this soup-ladle in possession of Charlotte Rand—I told her one day, when she was going to send it up, that our people used silver ladles, not the earthenware ones—I know this plate and dishes.
LEWIS BRAHAM . I was taken by the officer on the 5th of Jan.—Robert Rand was a tailor—I had seen his wife frequently come into his house in Waterloo-town, Bethnal-green.
CHARLOTTE RAND — GUILTY .— Confined One Year.
ROBERT RAND — NOT GUILTY .
(There was another indictment against Charlotte Rand.)
[trialAccount t18460202-411]
411. FREDERICK CLARK was indicted for stealing 1 pump, value 15 l., the goods of Daniel Watney, in a barge on the river Thames.—2nd COUNT, for stealing 83lbs. weight of copper, 14 l., and 20lbs. weight of metal, 1 l.; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN WILLIAMS . I am a lighterman, in the service of Daniel Watney, who has a wharf at Wandsworth—his premises all join—I had charge of a lighter belonging to him on the 3rd of Feb., 1845—I had a copper pump and a bucket there—I saw them safe between six and seven o'clock the night before, and next morning the barge was gone—I found it at Battersea Reach—I got on board, and missed the pump and bucket—in my judgment the copper now produced formed that pump and bucket.
CHARLES BOORE . I am shopman to Lambert and Son, brass-founders, New Cut, Lambeth. I received this metal bucket from Mrs. Somers on the 3rd of Feb., 1845—I delivered it to Reed on the 5th of Feb.
THOMAS SPINKS . In Feb., 1845, I was in the service of Mrs. Somers, a coppersmith. On the 3rd of Feb. I received some copper which has formed a pump—it was brought by Hosier and Hadley who have been convicted—there was a third man outside, who was dressed something like a coal-heaver—he
had very little whiskers—he was about the same size as the prisoner; but it he is the man, he is so altered that I cannot recognise him—I afterwards gave the copper pump to the officer—I think 2 l. 14 s. was paid for it.
JOHN MERRY ( police-constable V 70.) I received nine pieces of copper from Mr. Spinks, at Mrs. Somers's shop.
JOHN CHARLES HEATH SAUNDERS . I am a millwright in the employ of Mr. Watney—this bucket is his property.
THOMAS EMERSON ( police-sergeant V 16.) I was at the police-station at Clapham on the 12th of Jan., when the prisoner was brought in in custody—I told him what he was charged with—he said he was outside, While Richard Hosier and Thomas Hadley were taking these things—I read the warrant to him—he said nothing then—about a quarter of an hour after, when I went to the cell and gave him two sacks to cover over him, he said, "It was a bad job—I was with them, I shall get transported the same as they are," or "as well as they are" that he must take the rough with the smooth, he could not help it now—he said he had been living at all parts of London since that—I told him Hadley and Hosier were transported, one at Croydon, and one at the Central Criminal Court.
Cross-examined. Q. It appears there were two men tried for this crime? A. Yes—I am not aware that there is a reward for the apprehension of any person who committed that robbery—I never asked the foreman any questions—I did not take down the words the prisoner repeated—I have a good memory—I was not at the former trial—I do not know that the prisoner had been drinking—he had not the appearance of it.
HENRY JAMES ELLIOTT . On the 13th of Jan. I was locked up in the station—I was charged with spending some money, but I was not convicted—the Magistrate dismissed me by letting me pay the money—I was in the same cell where the prisoner was on the 13th—I said to him "What are you in here for?"—he said, "Stealing a pump"—he said, "There was a pump stolen from Mr. Watney's, at Wandsworth, twelve months ago"—I said, "Were you in the mess?"—he said, "Yes; the other two mates got transported ten years each: one was from Croydon, the other the Old Bailey," but, says he, "I hooked it; I was a full-whiskered man then, but I shaved my whiskers all off"—he said it was a bad job, he had no doubt but what he should get lagged for it, he must make his mind up to that; it was a bad job, he could not help it now—he said he should not have been taken if he had not come on that side of the water to help his sister move—that he had a bed on his head when the policemen came to him, but he gave the policemen a two hours' run, and then gave them an upper and a downer before they took him—I had no quarrel with him.
Cross-examined. Q. Will you repeat exactly what this man stated on the 13th of Jan.? A. I asked him what he was in there for—he asked me what I was in for—I told him I was never locked up in a gaol before—the money I spent was for work done at the Wandsworth Railroad—it belonged to me and some more men—they gave me orders to take it—the Magistrate allowed me my liberty on condition of repaying the money, because the men were agreeable to it—I have been at work at the reservoirs in Battersea-fields—I worked before that for Mr. Thornton, at Nine Elms—I do not know what I am to have for my trouble in coming here—I asked Mr. Paynter whether I should be paid my expenses—he said, "Yes, when you go to the Old Bailey."
ROBERT SMITH ( police-sergeant L 21.) I apprehended the prisoner on the 12th of Jan., in Broad-wall, Christ church, Surrey—he had a bed on his head—I had been looking after him for about three months—I asked if his name was Frederick Clarke—he denied it, and said it was William Bryant—I took
him to the gas-lamp, and recognised him—as we were going along, he ran away—I had a quarter of an hour's good hard run after him—he ran up a street which was no thoroughfare, and two gentlemen caught him.
JAMES WILLIAM GRIFFIN ( Thames-police inspector.) I have known the prisoner about eight years—he wore whiskers about twelve or fourteen months ago—I know both Hosier and Hadley—I have seen the prisoner in their company at different times—the last time was about two years ago, when they were on the river—I have been on the look-out for the prisoner for twelve months.
HENRY JOSEPH KING ( Thames police-constable, No. 30.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at this Court—( read Convicted 31 st Jan., 1842, and confined three months)—I was at the trial—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined One Year.

649. MARY ANN HASLETT was indicted for stealing 4 gowns, value 15 s.; 2 gown-bodies, 1 s.; 1 spoon, 3 s.; 1 pair of straps, 2 d.; 1 comforter, 3 d.; 1 bag, 1 s.; 2 habit-shirts, 1 s.; 3 yards of ribbon, 6 d.; 1 pair of cuffs, 4 d.; 1 cap, 9 d.; 1 shirt-frill, 3 d.; 3 pairs of stockings, 9 d.; 2 pairs of gloves, 6 d.; and 1 pair of stays, 2 s. 6 d.; the goods of Frederick Edwards, her master.
MARTHA EDWARDS . I am the wife of Frederick Edwards—we live in Stangate-street, Lambeth. The prisoner was in my service, and was going away—I missed several articles—When the prisoner was going I called her back, and took her up stairs—I told her I missed these things—she said she knew nothing about them—I said I could not let her go till I saw myself righted, and she should stay all night—I went down and wrote to her father—I asked her his address, and she gave me a false one—about twelve at night she slipped out—I gave information the next morning—I saw the things at the station—they were all mine.
JAMES THOMAS SIMONS ( police-constable L 125.) I went to the prisoner's father's, and found her there—these gowns were on the bed in the house where she was—I found her on the evening of the 23rd of Jan.—she had left her place at twelve the night before.
MART HOWARD . I searched the prisoner. I found on her a pair of stockings and the body of a dress, which her mistress identified.
Prisoner. My mistress gave me the stockings I had on.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Four Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18460202-650]
650. MARY ANN BOOTH was indicted for stealing 1 crown, the monies of Ann Giles, her mistress.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the monies of Henry Giles.
HENRY GILES . I live in Blackfriars-road, with my mother, Ann Giles. The prisoner was in our service since August last—I left a crown piece in my waistcoat pocket, in the top room, on the 15th of Jan., about eleven o'clock in the morning—when I came back, in about a quarter of an hour, the crown was gone—there were two or three notches on the edge of the crown—I came down from the room—I passed the prisoner, who was going up stairs, and as I went up again I passed her coming down—I got a policeman—when he came, the prisoner said she would go up stairs and find the five-shilling piece, if we would not go up with her—she would not tell us where it was—she said she would rather go to the station—I went up stairs—there is a trap door at top—I went up, and looked through some laths—the prisoner came up to
the room—she ran two or three times to see if anybody was coming after her—I then saw her stoop and take up a purse by the fire-place—I jumped down, took it from her, and gave it to the policeman.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What was in the purse? A. The crown piece, a pencil-case, and ring—I had not missed the pencilcase, but I had the ring—my mother has been confined to her room four years—we took the prisoner in because her father and mother were very poor—they had no bed to lie on—we allowed her wages every week—I had left ray waistcoat between the mattress and the bed—she had not seen me put it there.
JAMES STRATFORD ( police-constable L 50.) I was sent for—I followed the prisoner up to the room—the first thing I saw was the prisoner, and Mr. Giles dropping from the trap door—he gave me the purse—I found this crown in it—I asked the prisoner where she got it—she said she took the silver out of Master Henry's pocket, and the ring she found on the Monday week before, on the corner of the carpet.
HENRY GILES . This is the crown piece I had.
GUILTY . Aged 13.— Confined Three Weeks.
[trialAccount t18460202-651]
651. JOHN HODDINOTT was indicted for stealing 2 seals, value 19 s.; 1 thimble, 2 s.; 1 handkerchief, 3 s.; 27 keys, 7 s.; 3 sixpences, and 4 pence; the property of Edward Caldecott, from the person of Sarah Ann Caldecott.
SARAH ANN CALDECOTT . I am the wife of Edward Caldecott—he is cook at Mr. Ward's, in Bond-street—I live in Windmill-street, Lambeth. On the 20th of Jan., towards seven o'clock in the evening, I was on the back seat of a Dorking coach, passing along High-street, in the Borough—I turned round once or twice, and saw the prisoner hanging behind the coach—I took no notice of him at first, but he got up to the rail, and I felt my dress touched; and Mr. Cheeseman said to the prisoner, "That game won't do here, Jack," and said to me, "Mrs. Caldecott, your dress if cut"—I found my dress and pocket were cut through, and I missed the thimble and other articles stated—the coachman pulled up directly, and Mr. Cheeseman got down and ran after the prisoner—I had not been on the coach above three or four minutes—my pocket was all right when I got on the coach, I am quite sure—my pocket was cleared of everything but a penknife, which was between the lining of my pocket—here is my dress, which is cut, and my pocket also.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Had you known the prisoner before? A. No—I distinctly recognize, and am quite sure he is the person—I had not been in a crowd before that—I am sure this had not been done before I got up—I had taken my handkerchief out of my pocket after I got on the coach, and it was all right.
ALFRED CHEESEMAN . I am porter at the Golden Cross, Charing-cross. I was on the same coach with Mrs. Caldecott—I have known the prisoner about the Elephant and Castle for ten or twelve years—I turned, and observed him behind the coach—I said, "That game won't do here"—he jumped down momently and ran, with hit head ducked down—I stopped the coach immediately, and said to Mrs. Caldecott, "Your pocket is cut"—the coachman said, "What is the matter?"—I said. "This lady's pocket is cut"—he said, "Who did it?"—I said, "Mr. Hoddinott"—I knew the prisoner perfectly well, and gave information—he was taken afterwards
WILIAM MARTIN ( police-constable P 289.) I apprehended the prisoner at No. 8, New-street, Kent-road—I told him it was for cutting a lady's pocket, and stealing a bunch of keys and other articles.
GUILTY .** Aged 31.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
[trialAccount t18460202-652]
652. GEORGE THOMAS EMMETT was indicted for stealing 1 pair of spur-leathers, value 1 s., 6 d.; 1 leather strap, 1 s.; 1 bridle, 2 s. 6 d.; 3 hearinghooks, 2 s. 9 d.; 1 whip, 10 s.; 1 brush, 2 s. 6 d.; 1 box. 1 s.; 1 snuff-box, 6 d.; 1 stop-cock, 6 d.; and 2lbs. of brass, 1 s.; the goods of Samuel Hammond.—2nd COUNT of Allen Miles.
SAMUEL HAMMOND . I am messenger's assistant to the Court of Bankruptcy—on the 12th of Nov. I was put in possession of the goods and premises of a person named Davis, in High-street, Borough—the prisoner was employed by the broker to lot the goods—he came there on the 16th of Dec.—he had a blue bag in his hand—I afterwards saw him leave the premises with a bag in his hand, which appeared to be the same he had brought—I have examined the brass-work produced—It is such at is used by harnessmakers—I did not deliver to the prisoner, or suffer to be taken by him, any of this brass.
Prisoner. The property produced is in the inventory. Witness. We have not missed anything from the inventory, but I am not able to say whether these were in it or not—the sale had not taken place.
GEORGE WILD ( police-constable, M 94.) I saw the prisoner in a marinestore dealer's shop, in Union-street, on the 16th of Dec., with a blue bag in his hand, and a riding-whip in his right hand—he placed the bag on the counter, and took from it a brown paper, containing these rings and this brass-work—I went to him, told him I was an officer, and asked if they were his—he said, "Yes"—I asked where he worked—he declined telling me—he said he lived in May-fair—I said, "I must take possession of this, and take you, except you can tell me where you have been at work to-day"—on the road to the station, he said if I would go to No. 100, High-street, there was a person named Hammond, in possession under the Bankruptcy Court, and he would make it all right, for he gave him the brass, and told him to sell it to buy some porter for to-morrow—as we were going to the station, I missed the whip—I said, "Where is your whip?"—he said, "I have dropped it, but that is of no consequence"—I took him to the station, and was ordered to search him—he said I need not, he had nothing about him—I found this strap, this brass box, and these other things in his coat pocket—I said, "You said you had nothing"—he said, "Mr. Hammond gave me these; It is of no consequence."
Prisoner. Q. Do you say I offered them for sale? A. You placed them on the counter—the dealer referred to his book, and asked for your name and your card—I stated before the Magistrate that you said you had dropped the whip.
GEORGE DAVIS . I am a harness-maker, and live at No. 100, High-street, Borough. I have lately become bankrupt—Hammond was in possession of my premises—the prisoner was employed to assist in lotting the goods—I noticed his bringing a blue bag—he seemed to have his victuals in it, and two or three bottles—I saw him leave with his bag—on the following day I found a quantity of brass-work and other things in possession of the constable—I have every reason to believe they formed part of my stock—they were such as I used, and had in my premises—I do not recollect this strap, but I had a box like this, and this brush has my name on it—I know this brass gas-joint—I put it under the shop counter, that it might be sold for old metal—I know it because it has a hole through which the gas escaped—I did not give the prisoner permission to take any of these things from the premises—I gave him a pair of old gaiters just before he left—Mr. Hammond did not give him any of them—I had no reason to suppose he had anything but the remains of his food—this box is like the one I had, and which I had missed from my
premises—these other things are such as I used in my trade—I should think they are my property—some of these were in drawers—I suppose these had been left out of the lottings, or had been taken out of the lots.
Prisoner. I leave it entirely to your mercy.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY .— Confined Nine Months.

654. THOMAS JAMES GILL was indicted for stealing 2 pairs of trowsers, value 6 s., the goods of Samuel Shaw: and 1 jacket, value 5 s., the goods of Thomas Dobson; in a certain vessel in the navigable river Thames.
SAMUEL SHAW . I am master of the schooner Vigilant. On the 26th of Dec. she was lying at Simmons'-wharf, Tooley-street—the prisoner was my apprentice—I missed him on that day, and not ten minutes after he was gone I missed out of the cabin two pairs of canvass trowsers—they have not been found—I had four men on board beside the prisoner.
THOMAS DOBSON . I missed a jacket and a satin waistcoat from the forecastle of that vessel—this is my jacket—I had not lent it to the prisoner.
GEORGE WALKER JUDGE . I am a Thames police-inspector. I went to the prisoner's father's house, and found this jacket there—I apprehended the prisoner there—I told him he was charged with stealing a jacket, a waistcoat, and two pairs of trowsers—he said he had pawned them at a house in Whitechapel—he said Tom had lent him the things; he meant to get the waistcoat out, and give it to him.
JAMES GILL . The prisoner is my son. I delivered this jacket to the officer—my boy took it off in my house, and I took possession of it.
Prisoner's Defence. I asked Dobson if he would let me take the things on shore; he said, "Yes."
THOMAS DOBSON re-examined. I did not—it was not likely when I was going to sea.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18460202-655]
655. WILLIAM FROST and DAVID HOGG were indicted for stealing 1 tame fowl, value 2 s., the goods of John George Shaw Lefevre.
GEORGE ALLUM . I am servant to Mr. Wilcox, of Wimbledon-common. I saw the prisoners on the 14th of Jan., going towards Mr. Lefevre's fowls—Frost threw something at one of the fowls, and it dropped, and he took it up and walked off—there was something thrown down first for the fowls to eat.
JAMES YOUNG . I am gardener to Mr. Marston, who lives on Wimbledoncommon. I saw the prisoners that day—Hogg fed some fowls within a yard of Mr. Lefevre's premises, and Frost knocked one down—I could not exactly tell which took it away.
Frost. He said he did not know who threw it, and then the second time he said it was me. Witness. Mr. Fletcher took my statement down—I feel quite satisfied that what I stated the first day I did on the third—on the second day I was not asked a question.
Hogg. There are many persons keep fowls there, and this man was a
quarter of a mile from the place. Witness. It was a short quarter of a mile—I know all the fowls about there—I am sure they were Mr. Lefevre's.
WILLIAM TIPPING . I am gardener to Mr. John George Shaw Lefevre, of Wimbledon-common. I have the care of his fowls—on the morning of the 14th of Jan. I was gone to Croydon—when I came home in the evening I missed a light-coloured fowl—it was one of last year's fowls—I lost three fowls between the 11th and the 14th—they are principally the Dorking breed—I think we have got the best breed of Dorking fowls upon the common—the others are more of a mixed breed—the light-coloured one I lost was a greyish one.
RICHARD GOLDING ( police-Constable V 31.) I received information, and went on Thursday, the 15th of Jan., to Frost's house—I found both the prisoners there, and took them—I found in the room down stairs an uncooked shoulder of mutton, and three pieces of the chine of a sheep; and on moving a loose board in the floor I found a fowl's wing and feathers, another shoulder of mutton, not cut in a butcher-like manner, thirty-six snares, and two iron gins—on searching Hogg's pocket at the Police-court I found a quantity of small white feathers and some oats—I found in Frost's house two steel traps.
RICHARD STREATFIELD ( police-constable V 114.) I went with Golding to the prisoner's house—when the wing of the fowl was pulled up from under the floor, Frost took it up and put it into the fire—he said it was of no good now—it was the wing of a dark fowl—I took it out again.
Frost. I own I just put it between the bars, but I said not a word.
FROST — GUILTY . Aged 25.
HOGG — GUILTY . Aged 20.
Confined One Year.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18460202-656]
656. JOSEPH HASTINGS, MATTHEW GIBSON , and RICHARD BOXALL , were indicted for stealing 1 sack, value 6 d.; 2 tame geese, 5 s.; 8 live fowls, 13 s.; 1 saw, 1 s. 3 d.; and 2 walking-sticks, 3 s.; the goods of George Willis.
JOHN HAYTER . I am in the service of Mr. George Willis, at Hearn-hill. On the night of the 14th of Jan. his premises were broken into, he lost the geese, fowls, and other things stated—this saw I can identify—I put it in the tool-shed about five o'clock in the evening, and missed it the next morning between nine and ten o'clock—this is the saw, it is my master's, it is broken now—it was not broken when I feft it—the fowls and geese were kept close by the tool shed.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. How do you swear to this saw? A. One or two of the teeth are broken out, and two or three years ago I broke the handle in this place—I had used it the day, it was left—the point of the tooth is broken off.
CHARLES ANSTRIDGE . I am cow-man to Mr. George Willis. At a quarter before five o'clock on the night of the 14th of Jan. I looked up my master's premises—I locked up the fowl-houses—there were eight fowls—the geese were not locked up—they were close by, round the pound—this sack was in the cow-house, which is close to the fowl-house—I came there at half-past six o'clock the next morning, and they were all gone—I examined the geese and fowls and sack which were found—they were my master's—these walkingsticks I know—they were standing against the cow-house door.
DAVID WILKES ( police-constable P 94.) At a quarter before ten o'clock at night, on the 14th of Jan., I was on duty near Dulwich—I met Hastings with the other two prisoners—they bid me good night—about a quarter after ten o'clock I went to the end of my beat, and I had information they had
been in Mr. Clark's premises—we searched, but could not find them—at a quarter to twelve o'clock they came on my beat again—I followed them, thinking to find my brother officer, but I could not—I secured Hastings—the geese and fowls were found across the road opposite where I saw the prisoners cross the road—they were going in a direction from Mr. Willis, and were opposite his premises—I did not find the poultry—they were dead when I saw them, and had marks of blood on them.
Cross-examined. Q. About what time did you see the prisoners first? A. About a quarter to ten o'clock—I saw them again on my beat at a quarter before twelve, crossing from one side of the road to the other, directly opposite where the property was found—Mr. Willis's is 800 or 900 yards from where I saw them cross.
JOHN WOOD ( police-constable P 131.) About twelve o'clock that night I saw Hastings and two other men, but I cannot identify the others—about as hour afterwards I found eight fowls and two geese, a sack, two walking-sticks, and a saw—I pointed out to Hayter where I found them.
GEORGE RUSSEN ( police-constable P 529.) About nine o'clock in the morning I took the shoes off Hastings at the station, and went to Mr. Willis with them—I traced foot-marks from Mr. Willis's to within three yards of where this property was found—the foot-marks corresponded with Hastings' shoes.
Cross-examined. Q. You prove the shoe-marks? A. Yes, these are Hastings' shoes—I found the shoe-marks from the prosecutor's premises across another gentleman's premises to within three or four yards of where the property was found—I put the shoe in the marks and I found they actually corresponded—there was one place where he had trod in some cowdung and here are the remains of the cow-dung left on the shoe—Mr. Hayter was with me.
JOHN HAYTER ( re-examined.) I saw the officer examine these marks—they correspond with one place in particular, in the cow-dung—I saw the mark in that cow-dung before the officer put the shoe into the hole, and it corresponded with the cow-dung.
Cross-examined. Q. That is, the cow-dung on the ground corresponded with the other cow-dung? A. Yes, there was the impression of part of the cow-dung on the shoe—previous to the policeman putting the shoe in, I saw the same dung on the shoe—the officer put the shoe into the impression of the cow-dung—I cannot remember whether it was a wet night.
COURT. Q. Did you observe this footmark in the dung before the shoe was put into it? A. Yes, there were marks of the cow-dung removed from the earth, and the dung appeared to be on the same spot on the shoe—it appeared to correspond exactly—the officer called my attention to it.
JURY. Q. Were there footmarks of other persons? A. There were footmarks of more than one—the cow-dung is here on the inside of the shoe—there were other footmarks near to where the property was found, but there was a bank near there, and I could not trace the marks further—the feet had slipped.
COURT to DAVID WILKES. Q. Are you able to identify the other two men who were with Hastings; you did not before the Magistrate? A. Yes, the prisoners are the two men—a female told me their names, and that induced me to look at their faces.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460202-657]
657. JOSEPH HASTINGS and WILLIAM HARSANT were indicted for stealing 4 tame ducks, value 6 s. the property of James Gardner.
HENRY BULLEN . I am in the employ of Mr. James Gardner, at Camberwell—on the 10th of Jan., about four o'clock, I saw his five ducks safe in a pen—I went the next morning a little after seven o'clock, and there was only one duck—I observed some footmarks close up to the place where the ducks were, and in the pen—some shoes were examined in my presence by putting the shoes in the tracks in a new piece of ground—I looked at the tracks first and at the shoes, and they corresponded exactly—nothing could be more exact—these are the two pairs of shoes—we compared them three days afterwards, but there had been no wet—the marks were quite fresh.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. You were with the policeman when you examined the tracks? A. Yes—I am perfectly sure about the footmarks—there were a few others beside those, but not on the bed where these were—these were in the upper garden—there were the same marks in the pen, but we did not try them—we only tried them about 100 yards from the pen, and that was three days after this occurrence.
COURT. Q. Were there traces from the place where you tried the marks up to the pen? A. Yes, and they appeared exactly the same.
GEORGE ROSSEN ( police-sergeant P 29.) I got these two pairs of shoes from the two prisoners on the morning of the 15th of Jan.—I compared them with the marks in Mr. Gardner's garden—I examined, as far as my eye could, the traces of the foot-marks from the pen to the garden, and to the best of my belief they were the same.
Cross-examined. Q. In every mark that you examined did you put the shoe down? A. No—in everyone that I thought was the same mark I put the shoe down.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460202-658]
658. MATTHEW GIBSON and RICHARD BOXALL were indicted for stealing 4 tame fowls, value 10 s., the goods of Joseph Kimpton; and that Gibson had been before convicted of felony.
JOSEPH KIMPTON . I live in Sussex-place, Old Kent-road. I had four fowls—I shut them up between two and three o'clock in the afternoon, on the 30th of Jan.—the next morning they were gone, between eight and nine o'clock—the policeman found them—he had the four which I had lost at the police-court.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. Have you any doubt of them? A. Not the least.
WILLIAM MORTON ( police-constable P 289.) About eight o'clock on the night of the 30th of Jan. I met the prisoners in the Old Kent-road—I had been in search of them for a week on another charge—I took Boxall, but did not get more than twenty yards before he got away—I took him again, and took him across the road to a cheesemonger's shop—he had a basket on his head—I threw it off his head, and my wife, who was close behind, called out, "Here are some fowls in the basket"—I told her to take care of them—Gibson was brought over to me by another officer—I handcuffed the prisoners together, and asked them where they had got the fowls—Boxall said "You would b—y well like us to tell you, should not you?"—two fowls were found in Gibson's pocket—the fowls were shown to the prosecutor—he identified them—they were just killed, and warm—the prisoners had got about 300 yards from the prosecutor's.
SARAH MORTON . I am the wife of William Morton. I saw the basket fall off Boxall's head—it contained two of these fowls.
THOMAS CONNOR ( police-constable M 252.) I produce a certificate of
Gibson's former conviction at Newington—( read Convicted 17 th March, 1845, and confined fourteen days and whipped)—the prisoner is the person.
GIBSON — GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.
BOXALL — GUILTY .— Confined Nine Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
