659. ELLEN DOOLEY was indicted for stealing 1 guard-chain, value 4 l.; and 1 watch, 16 l.; the goods of James Bruton, her master.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
ANN BRUTON . I live at No. 11, London-road. The prisoner was my service—on the morning of the 27th of Jan. I went up stairs and saw my husband's watch safe in the bed-room—the prisoner was at that time gone out on an errand—I left the room, leaving the watch on the table, and put the clothes-basket at the room door, ready to be brought down—I went down and saw the prisoner put her hand to the mat at the street door, in order to shut it—I told her to take a can of water up stairs, put it down at the room door, and bring down the clothes-basket, but not to go into the room—she came down and I desired her to go for some honey to a shop over the way—I stood by the clothes-basket till she came back—she gave it into my hand and I sent her then to Mr. Bruton's mother's, No 30, Joiner-street—she went there to wash our clothes and took the clothes-basket with her—she left me about half-past ten o'clock, and was to remain there till dinner time—I went up stairs immediately, and my husband's watch was gone—I went up I am sure in one minute—there were other persons in the house—Mr. Bailey and his mother and sister, and one servant belonging to the house, whose name is Emma—she was in the coffee-room at the lower part of the house—I am not aware whether she left the house about that time—I very seldom saw the girl—I did not hear the street door shut or open during the time I was going up stairs—I was going to dress—I went straight to the table where the watch was—I am confident there was not time for any person to have gone out of the house in that short time—I could hear the street door shut or open in any room in the house—I am confident no one went out—when I missed the watch I went to Mr. Bruton, who was in the coffee-room, and he went to Joiner-street—I saw the watch produced by Mordy, the next night.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. Joiner-street is close by your house, is it not? Q. Yes, I should think not five minutes' walk—we have lodgers in the house—it is a coffee-house—we occupy the first floor, and Mr. Davis keeps the house—his sister, Mrs. Penny, and her husband occupy the floor above us—Emma is Mrs. Penny's servant as well as Mr. Davis's—a person can go out through the coffee-shop as well as through the private door—the door could not be closed silently, there is a latch that would not allow it—there is no person living on the third floor—the prisoner had been in my service about a fortnight—the can of water was for use in the bed-room, and the basket was outside the door—I was generally in the habit of leaving this watch about till Mr. Bruton dressed, and the prisoner had always access to the bed-room during those times—it was twenty-five minutes past ten o'clock when I left the watch.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. During the time the prisoner was going out you were on the staircase, down which any person must have come who was coming from that room? A. Yes—there was no other mode of getting from that room than down that staircase—I stood opposite the coffee-room door—any person who went out of the private door or through the coffee-room, must have passed me.
JAMES BRUTON . On the 27th of Jan. I was reading in the coffee-room, about half-past ten o'clock—I was knocked for from the room, by Mrs. Bruton—in consequence of what she said, I went to Joiner-street immediately I had arranged my dress—it was then within a minute or two of half-past ten o'clock—I went to No. 30, Joiner-street—I went into the kitchen, and saw the prisoner by the copper-fire—I said to her, "Ellen, have you seen my watch?"—she said, "No"—I went home late the next day, and my watch was lying on the table in the house where I live.
ANN BRUTON re-examined. This is the watch—it was brought back on the Wednesday night at past eleven o'clock, by Mordy—it was not going then—it was going when it went away.
JAMES BRUTON re-examined. This is the watch—my name is engraved on the back of it—I saw it in the bedroom before I quitted it to go into the coffee-room—I am not aware that any injury had happened to it.
Cross-examined. Q. How many minutes elapsed before you got to Joiner-street? A. I should say I was not two minutes going from one house to the other—I ran very fast—in three minutes you could walk the distance easily—the prisoner appeared to me as if she was stooping down—I did not accuse her of taking the watch—I did not believe it—I said if she would tell me the honest truth, I would be merciful to her—she said she would confess if she was guilty—I told her more than once that I would be merciful, Which I should have been.
COURT. Q. Did she come back to your house? A. I brought her back with me from Joiner-street—she never left my house again, and had no opportunity of taking the watch to Joiner-street, except she took it before quitted the house.
WILLIAM MORDY . I live at No. 30, Joiner-street. I remember seeing some woman in the kitchen of that house, on Tuesday, the 27th of Jan.—I will not be positive about the time—I think it must be when I came home to breakfast—my regular time for coming home is half-past eight—sometimes it is nine—I had not breakfasted that morning—it was when I came in and opened the door—I do not think it was as late as ten—I saw the woman where the copper is—on the Wednesday after I left my work, my mistress opened the door and told me something—in consequence of what I heard, I went to the copper in the kitchen, and found the watch up the chimney flue—there is a flue from the kitchen chimney to the other—somebody had put it up there, I suppose—it was placed in the intermediate passage, on a brick that lies flat between the copper flue and the chimney, about eighteen inches from the hob of the kitchen chimney—I had been searching everything in the kitchen—I then placed the light on the hob, looked up the chimney, and saw a dirty rag—I put my arm up, pulled it down, and found the watch in it—I took it to Mrs. Bruton.
Cross-examined. Q. I think you say it was about nine o'clock you saw this woman in the kitchen? A. I would not be positive to a few minutes—I am certain it was before ten o'clock—I found the watch on the Wednesday evening, in the kitchen where I had seen the woman.
JAMES PERRY ( police-sergeant L 20.) I took the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. You have known her from infancy? A. Yes, and never knew any charge against her.
COURT to ANN BRUTON. Q. Did any person leave your house to go to Joiner-street, between the time you missed the watch, and the evening of the next day? A. No—I did not send any one there, and no one quitted the house for the purpose of going there.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460202-600a]
600a. WILLIAM SIMMONDS was indicted for stealing 1 gas-fitting, called a main gas cock, value 3 s.; the goods of William James Shannon; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM JAMES SHANNON . I am a hair-dresser, and live at No. 134, Union-street, Borough. On Monday morning, the 19th of Jan., the prisoner came to my house—he said he had come from the gas company to put a cap on the main pipe—he employed himself at the gas-pipe, and went away—I was without gas some days, and when the inspector of the gas company came I missed the supply cock.
ANN CORBETT . I am a gas-fitter—the prisoner brought me a brass supplycock last Friday fortnight—he asked me to give him 9 d. for it, I said I did not want it—he said had I got 6 d.—I gave him 6 d.—he called again, and I said I would rather not have it—he said he would bring the 6 d., and have it back, but he did not, and I gave it to the officer.
JOSEPH TAYLOR ( police-constable M 56.) I received charge of the prisoner—he said he was sent by an inspector to cut off the gas, but he did not know his name, nor what company he belonged to—he told me I should find the cock at Corbett's—I produce it.
Prisoner. I was sent by an inspector; I passed the prosecutor's door three or four times, he called to me, and I said, "I know what you want, you want the brass tap that was on the pipe;" he said, "Yes, where is it?" I told him, and I said, "You shall have that, or a new one;" he told me to stop a minute; and he brought a policeman.
COURT to WILLIAM JAMES SHANNON. Q. Was anything the matter with the gas-cock? A. No—there was a dispute about paying for the supply of gas in advance—the man called, and I refused to pay it—the prisoner was waiting outside—he came in, and said he came from the inspector to take my gas off—he did not say he came to take the brass supply-cock away—that did not belong to the company—it is mine.
Prisoner. I worked for the Metropolitan Gas Company, in the factory, for two years; I cannot say whether the man belonged to the Metropolitan or the Phœnix, but I was called by the inspector, and told to put a stop-cap on, which I did. Witness. He brought this iron cap, took the brass main-tap away, and screwed this on the pipe—I was eight days without a light.
ANN CORBETT re-examined. This iron cap that the prisoner left is worth about 6 d.—the brass supply-cocks cost 1 s. 8 d., or 1 s. 10 d.
Prisoner. I left it with her till such time as I brought the sixpence back; I told her I would call and fetch it in a few days. Witness. No, he was to call again for the other money, and when he came he said he would bring the sixpence, and fetch it.
Prisoner. When I went to the prosecutor's house, he told me he was going to have the gas put on in a different way.
WILLIAM JAMES SHANNON re-examined. Yes, I did, if the place suited my business—the prisoner did not tell me he was going to cut off the supply-cock.
THOMAS CURTIS . I am an inspector of nuisances to the paving trust—I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction at this Court—( read Convicted the 3 rd of January, 1842, confined one year.)
GUILTY . Aged 49.— Confined Six Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18460202-661]
661. THOMAS WAITE and EDWARD RAYNES were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of Jan. 25 rose trees, value 3 l.; 21 peach trees, 1 l. 10 s.; and 3 cytisus trees, 7 s. 6 d.; in a garden adjoining the dwelling-house of Joseph Fryer.—2nd COUNT, in a certain nursery-ground.
JOSEPH FRYER . I am a nurseryman, and live in Camberwell New-road. On Saturday morning, the 17th of Jan., about a quarter to seven o'clock, I opened the gates of my nursery, which adjoins my dwelling-house—my house is in the nursery—I missed thirty standard rose trees, twenty-one peach trees, and three cytisus, which I had seen safe at six the previous evening, growing in the nursery—they were in an inclosed place—I gave information to Kendall, and was with him on the Wednesday following, when the trees were found behind a house in which the prisoners live—they said they resided there—I found a label on one of the peach trees, which I knew—I can swear to it by that—there had been labels on the other trees, but they had been torn off—the trees themselves exactly correspond in size, number, and quality with those I lost—the peach trees were maiden trees, only grafted one year—I had had them from a gentleman in the country on the previous Thursday—I saw them all put into my ground, and I attended to them myself—I could swear to the trees being mine, independent of the label—these now produced are them—Raynes had worked for me for a short time about two years ago.
Raynes. Q. What character did I bear while with you? A. I never heard anything against your honesty at that time.
PETER KENDALL ( police-sergeant P 1.) On Wednesday, the 21st of Jan., about twelve o'clock in the day, I went to a cottage in Wheeler's-fields—I found Raynes sitting by the fire, and Waite behind the door—I said, "Hollo!" and he repeated the same word—Waite said, "I know what you are coming about, you are come about those rose trees; a man named Piper was to give me 5 s. to try and sell them"—he said he did not know where Piper was, or where the trees were—Raynes said he knew nothing about it—I took them to the station, took their shoes from their feet, went to Mr. Fryer's ground, and compared them with the marks in the ground—they all four corresponded with the marks there—I put the shoes in the marks—I noticed the marks before I put the shoes in—they corresponded with marks going and coming from where the trees were taken—I afterwards went to the ground near where the prisoners resided, and there I saw similar footmarks—I tracked them till I came to a trench covered over with dung, I took that away, and there found the trees produced, on one of which was this label.
EDWARD DENYER . I am a nurseryman at Brixton. On the 20th of Jan. Waite came to me and offered some rose trees for sale—I had never seen him before—I asked how he came by them—he said he was ordered to sell them by a man named Brown—there are several Browns who are nurserymen—he did not say where Brown lived—I asked the price—he said they were very fine, and had very large heads; he was instructed to sell them at five guineas a hundred—I said that was too much, I would give him 9 s. a dozen, if they were correctly come by—I had previously heard of Mr. Fryer's loss, and I sent information to him—I asked Waite his name and address, and this is what I wrote down, "Waite, gardener, Cambridge-road, Peckham, 33 standard rose trees, 3 standard cytisus, &c."—he brought me the list—I did not see the trees.
Waite. That is my father's address; I used to lodge with him.
GEORGE BISH . I am a gardener, and live in Garden Cottage, Wheeler's-fields, the house the prisoners were taken from—they were lodging there at that time—on the Saturday week about half-past seven o'clock in the morning, I got up to go to my work and perceived some rose trees in a yard at the side of the cottage—the prisoners could get to that yard—I said to them "You have got some rose trees here," they said they had got them from the
country, and were going to have a lot more—I returned about twelve o'clock and the prisoners were then in custody.
Raynes. Q. Are you sure it was on the Saturday morning you saw them? A. Yes—on the 17th of Jan.—I only saw rose trees.
JOHN PEAKALL . I am an ink manufacturer, and live in Cottage-place, Lambeth-walk—I rent the piece of garden-ground for my own amusement, where the trees were found—I was there on the Sunday, and the trees were not there then.
PETER KENDALL re-examined. I went to the address Waite gave Mr. Denyer—he does not live there.
Waite's Defence. On the 17th or 18th of Jan. a person named Piper came to my cottage and asked if I bought roses; he said that he had some from the country and should have a great many more; I said I was not a buyer; he said perhaps I could sell them for him and he would give me 5 s. commission, and he left the list with me; I went to several nurserymen, and to Mr. Denyer, but could not sell them; I told Piper so, and he said he could sell them himself; he is out of the way, and is not to be found now; the trees were found in our open ground, and there are five or six cottages on the ground.
Raynes's Defence. I was sitting at home when Piper came; I had never seen him before; Waite went out to him, and was talking about a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes; when he came in he had the list in his hand, and said he had got a job.
MR. PEAKALL re-examined. The ground where the trees were found is inclosed, and my fence was broken down to enable them to get in and bring them there.
WAITE — GUILTY .
RAYNES — GUILTY .
Confined Six Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoners.)
[trialAccount t18460202-662]
662. ALBORN YOUNG was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of Jan., 13 dwts. 7 grains of gold, value 2 l. 10 s.; the goods of William Seymour and another, his masters.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
WM. SEYMOUR . I am in partnership with my son James Edward Seymour, as gold-beaters, at Long-acre—we have also an establishment at Peckham, where we employ apprentices—the prisoner was employed there as foreman—the witness Isaacs is an apprentice of ours—we supply the prisoner with the necessary tools and gold to work, five beatings at a time, 2oz. 7dwts. in the beating—it is first beaten out, and then made into leaf—a certain quantity of what is called shurf comes off—that is preserved and delivered to me as well as the leaves, and is melted—in Nov. last I delivered the prisoner, five of these rolls—I did not receive any of it back—I applied to him for it, and on the 21st of Jan. I ordered him to get his work up and give it me—I went on the following Monday to Peckham, and asked if his work was up—he said, "not quite, I shall not long about it"—(he had asked me on the previous Wednesday to lend him money, which I had declined)—he then said, "I am a beating and a half deficient, I have done wrong, look over it, and I will work it out"—I said no—I examined the work, and discovered a deficiency of 3 dwts. more than a beating and a half—I sent for a constable and gave him in charge.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Had he a child lying dead at that time? A. He had, and he has one child besides—there is what its called stone dirt, part of which is a perquisite of the workman—it is the sweepings out of
the stove—the prisoner had seven or eight persons employed under him, some are girls and women—about five persons would be employed under him to do these five beatings—he might have eight or nine—Isaacs has been in our employment about two months—he had worked for us before, in Longacre, about two years back—I do not know what he was doing in the meantime—he came down and asked for work, and we employed him—he made no communication to me till the day he was about to be sworn.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did Isaacs behave honestly and respectably when with you before? A. I believe there was some little thingamy or fall out between him and my son—the prisoner did not attribute his deficiency to Isaacs or anybody.
GEORGE ISAACS . I am a journeyman gold-beater, and work for Mr. Seymour, at Peckham—on the 21st of Jan. I went with the prisoner to several public-houses, and afterwards went home with him to the factory, about half-past twelve—he said to me, "Will you work all night? I am going to work"—I said I would—we went into the shop, and he said, "I shall melt my stone-dirt"—he melted it, and there was 3dwts. 6 grains—he said, "That won't do, I must have more"—he then went to his box in which his shruf was kept, took some gold out, weighed it, put it in, and melted it—there was 13 dwts. 7 grains, to the best of my recollection—he said that would do to pay for the funeral of his child, and get his wife a black gown—he told me on the Wednesday that he had asked Mr. Seymour to lend him 2 l., and he had refused, and he then remarked, "Well, he has refused to lend me the 2 l., I will have 2 l. worth out of the old b—"—he went to town at four o'clock next day with his wife, and said he was going to sell the gold—I saw him again on Friday morning, and he asked me to go to Nunheadcemetery with him about a piece of ground—at we were coming back he told me he had sold the gold, he had taken it to Mr. Groser first, and he refused to buy it, as it was between the lights, and he said, to the best of my recollection, that he had sold it to Mr. Collingridge—I could not swear to the name, but it ended with a ridge, and was in Wilderness-row—he said he got 2 l. 7 s. 6 d. for it—he said to me, "I hope you will keep it dark, for if you come it I shall get lagged, and I should look pretty in chains"—I did not say anything about it on that account till I was on my oath before the Magistrate.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you worked for a Mr. Laws? A. Yes—I cannot tell why I left him, it is so long ago—I belive something was said about some silver concealed behind the weights—I was discharged on suspicion of that—I was discharged by Mr. Seymour's son on account of the work not turning out well—I did not take advantage of the prisoner being partially intoxicated, and melt this gold myself—I swear that—I sat down and smoked my pipe for about an hour, and had some coffee, and then went to bed—the dead child was in the next room—I was not astonished to hear such language as keeping it dark, and bring lagged, because I have heard it often before among gold-beaters—Mr. Seymour took me before the Magistrate—he said, "I want you to speak the truth, and nothing but the truth, and I hope you will answer"—I did not go till I was compelled.
WILLIAM GROSER . I am a gold refiner, and live in Red Lion-street, clerkenwell—on the 21st or 22nd of Jan. I remember a short man cominginto my shop, about five o'clock, between the lights—he was about the heightof the prisoner, and his general appearance—I could not see his face distinctly—I was very busy at the other end of the premises, and finding he was a customer I did not like I got rid of him immediately—he put down a grain of gold—pushed it from me saying, "This will not suit me, neither the time of night nor the article"—he said something about lighting up—I said, "You
may bring it in the morning, if you want me to purchase it," and I retired and left him.
Cross-examined. Q. You cannot say whether it was the prisoner or not? A. I cannot.
THOMAS JENKINS DUNNELL . I am the prosecutor's son-in-law, and occasionally look after his business at Peckham—the prisoner has the sole charge of the gold delivered at that workshop—in Jan. I examined the gold delivered there, and found a deficiency of 3oz. 13dwts., which is worth about 15 l.—I heard Mr. Seymour ask him if his work was up—he said, "No, not quite—I am a beating and a half deficient—I have done wrong—if you will look over it, I will work it out"—I went and fetched a policeman.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe Mr. Seymour said, "He means he will work it further into me?" A. Yes—at that time we had not ascertained any deficiency, only what he stated himself—the work was given to other parties to finish.
JOHN TAPLIN . I finished this work—it weighed loz. only—it ought to have weighed 4oz. 14dwts.
SAMUEL WRIGHT ( policeman.) I took the prisoner into custody.
GUILTY .— Confined Twelve Months.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, 23 RD OF FEBRUARY.
